- **Now:** the badge renders with the request button beneath it, exactly as the `applied`, `refused` and `failed` branches already did. The badge still reports the last request honestly; it is no longer the only thing on screen.
- **Two tests were passing while asserting nothing.** `admin-device-report-action.test.tsx` checked `queryByRole("button", { name: /request report/i })` was null — but once `latest` is truthy the button reads "Request **again**", so the regex could not have matched either way. They now assert the button is present by its real label. A test that pins a bug is worse than no test, which is why this bullet exists.

### A-240 — `movement-types.ts` claimed pull.ts never replays a reconciliation movement, which is false on every device but one
- **Closed:** 2026-10-20. A record rather than a bug: the comment was rewritten in the same change that found it and no code defect remained. Kept because the false claim is why the A-239 doubling went unnoticed.
- **Found:** 2026-10-11. Corrected in the same change; recorded because the false claim is what made the A-239 doubling invisible.
- **Was:** `client/lib/db/movement-types.ts:6` read "pull.ts never replays it as a local delta". `pull.ts:403-423` has no `movement_type` check — and says so in its own comment — so the row's delta is applied on every device that receives it fresh. The non-replay is a property of the **originating** device only, produced by `storeReconciliationMovementLocally()` seeding the row `_synced = 1` so that device's pull takes the no-delta UPDATE branch. `client/AGENTS.md:1625-1646` already described this correctly, so the constant's docblock contradicted the architecture doc on a load-bearing path.
- **Status:** comment rewritten. Left here as the record of why A-239 went unnoticed; no code defect remains.

### A-214 — the 146 fabricated `sync_reconciliation` movements from the 2026-10-10 Health Sync run have been reversed
- **Closed:** 2026-10-20. The repair ran on production on **2026-10-10 17:58** with `clamped: 0, drifted: 0, skipped: 0` — a clean apply needing no physical count — and a report-mode run on 2026-10-11 confirmed nothing un-reversed remains.
- **Two threads stay open elsewhere and are deliberately not duplicated here:** the prevention side (Health Sync's device-quantity path being switched off rather than removed) is **A-213**, and the one-device tail (the asserting device that will double 144 products when it pulls the reversals) is **A-239**. Both are still in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md`; read them there.
- **Found:** 2026-10-10, after it happened in production. See A-213 for the design flaw this is the live instance of.
- **What happened:** at `2026-10-10 08:53:29` a single Health Sync run wrote **144 `sync_reconciliation` movements totalling −13,104 units** on store `571582a9`, zeroing products the log says held real stock (`vitamin c white counting` −1,626 against a log of 1,626; `fesolate` −1,528; `folic acid counting` −1,405), plus 2 movements (−18) on store `68e4c0e5`. Activity log confirms one run per store, same second, under the owner's account. It was pressed by a human: at the time the only callers were the Settings → Data button (`hooks/use-settings-sync.ts`) and the DevTools hook — nothing triggered it automatically. That button has since been removed (A-213), leaving the DevTools hook as the only caller.
- **Cause, and the code fixes that shipped:** a succeeded sync round is not a complete one, so a device mid-rebuild after `forceFullResync()` read 0 for every not-yet-rebuilt batch and asserted those zeros. The pre-flight now requires a complete movement-log window, the endpoint records the asserting device, and Health Sync itself is refused outright — see `docs/FIXED_BUGS.md` A-214 and A-213. **Those changes stop it happening again; they do not undo what happened.**
- **Unproven observation worth keeping (2026-10-12).** The store-`68e4c0e5` half of this incident was **2 movements totalling −18 units** — the same two numbers as A-248's **2 products / 18 units** mis-stamped from store `571582a9` into `68e4c0e5`. The tempting reading is that the store-2 Health Sync run found only those two batches to disagree about *because* their products had been re-stamped into that store, i.e. A-248 is why this incident reached store 2 at all. It is plausible but **not established**: the client sends its reconcile payload scoped by `stock_batches.store_id` (store 1 for these rows) while `SyncController::reconcileQuantities()` groups by the owning *product's* `store_id` (store 2 at the time), so the two can disagree in exactly this way — but nobody has traced the actual payload. Recorded because if A-214 is ever revisited, "why did it touch the second store at all" has a candidate answer, and because the two numbers matching by chance across one owner on one day would be a coincidence worth doubting.
- **Repair applied `2026-10-10 17:58:23` UTC**, both stores, in one pass: 144 movements / **+13,104 units** on `571582a9` and 2 / **+18** on `68e4c0e5`, with `clamped: 0, drifted: 0, skipped: 0` — a clean apply needing no physical count. A report-mode run on 2026-10-11 confirms nothing un-reversed remains. **The cloud total for `571582a9` is ~50,977 units across 2,626 batches both before the incident and after the repair** — pinned by `DRX-HO4QM3TWU`'s 09/10 report, which recorded `server_quantity_sum` 50,977 a day *before* the incident. That figure is the yardstick for reading any device's fingerprint, and it rules out the plausible-looking misreading that 50,977 was the zeroed total (which would have put the pre-incident figure at 64,081 and implied every device was still zeroed). Command reference: `php artisan sync:repair-health-sync-reconciliation` (reports by default, writes only with `--apply`, `--store=<id>` narrows it) reverses each of the 146 movements with a compensating `sync_reconciliation_reversal` movement carrying `reference_id` = the row it cancels, and sets each batch to **its stored quantity minus the bad deltas** — a delta reversal that never sums the movement log, so post-incident sales, deliveries and counts are kept rather than overwritten (they are already in the stored figure), and an A-148 batch with no movement rows is restored instead of being written back to zero (`docs/FIXED_BUGS.md` A-226, which is the defect the first version of this arithmetic had). It is idempotent, bounded to the run's own write-time window `2026-10-10 08:53:00`–`08:54:00` (A-216 — an upper bound alone also caught the legitimate 2026-10-02 A-148 restoration), reports any un-reversed reconciliation outside that window rather than hiding it, and is safe to run during trading hours: a batch a till moves underneath the repair is abandoned whole (no quantity write, no reversal), listed in the output, and finished by the next run. See `laravel-server/AGENTS.md`, "Health Sync zeroing repair". **What remains:** the founder must run it (report mode first) against production, and devices only converge on their next sync — a device that is missing a batch row entirely replays the reversal only once that batch arrives, which takes the ~10 rounds `docs/FIXED_BUGS.md` A-176b describes. Any batch the report flags as clamped still needs a physical count, and `units_restored` now reports the change actually written to each batch rather than the size of the reversal. This bullet is the repair side only; the prevention side (the pre-flight and the endpoint itself) is still open above.
- **Contributing factor worth recording:** a Force Full Resync had been recommended minutes earlier to clear a separate problem, and the Health Sync control sat directly beneath it in Settings → Data. A destructive action adjacent to a routine one, with no guard of its own, is a trap the product sets rather than an operator error. (That adjacency is gone: the Health Sync card was removed on 2026-10-10, so Force Full Resync now stands alone in that group.)

### A-288 — the `store_id` backfill ran, and every row it could not attribute turned out to be abandoned demo data
- **Closed:** 2026-10-20. The entry already described itself as resolved in practice and was never moved out of `KNOWN_BUGS.md`; this is that move, with no change to the record below.
- **One bullet below is latent, not active:** `requested_products` has no owner column, so a NULL-`store_id` row there could never be attributed by any derivation — but **zero such rows exist today** (the 2026-10-19 production run reported none). Nothing to do until one appears, which is why it is not filed as an open bug.
- **Found:** 2026-10-18, while building the per-table agreement fingerprint (`docs/DEVICE_TABLE_AGREEMENT.md`). Pre-existing data asymmetry, surfaced rather than caused by the new instrument.
- **Mostly fixed 2026-10-18** by `sync:backfill-store-ids` — see `laravel-server/AGENTS.md`, "`store_id` backfill", for the mechanism, the per-table derivation and its confidence. This entry stays open only for the residue below.
- **Location:** `App\Console\Commands\BackfillStoreIds::DERIVATIONS` against `CountsSyncedTableRows::scopeToStore()` and `SyncController::applyPullTenantScope()`, which filter on `store_id` for the 20 directly-scoped tables.
- **What's wrong, originally:** the device fills `store_id` on every legacy row it holds; the server's migrations never backfilled the column for the 11 tables of `add_store_id_to_domain_tables`. `NULL` never matches `where('store_id', ...)`, so such a row is counted locally, excluded in the cloud, and — since the pull uses the same filter — can never be delivered to any device either. `.agents/AGENTS.md` §11 "scoping filters are a contract too" verbatim. The six child tables are immune: the server scopes them through their parent and treats their own `store_id` as non-authoritative everywhere.
- **What the backfill cannot do, which is why this is still open:**
  - **`requested_products` has no owner column at all** — no `user_id`, no FK to anything store-scoped. Its NULL rows can never be attributed by any derivation and the command reports them untouched. If any exist, that table's agreement delta will not reach zero and the rows stay invisible to the sync engine permanently. Fixing it needs a signal that does not exist today (e.g. stamping `created_by` going forward, then attributing by that).
  - **A multi-store owner's rows are refused on purpose.** Where the owning store cannot be derived with certainty the row keeps its NULL and is counted as unattributable rather than guessed — a row stranded invisible is bad, a row handed to the wrong pharmacy is far worse.
- **Blast radius MEASURED on production, 2026-10-19 — and the residue is not a data gap.** `sync:backfill-store-ids --apply` attributed **594 `activity_logs` rows** and nothing else; **4,382 rows stayed NULL**, of which 4,332 are `activity_logs` and exactly **50 are business rows** (10 products, 22 stock_movements, 9 categories, 3 purchase_orders, 2 customers, 1 each of suppliers/expenses/prescriptions/returns).
- **All 50 belong to one user — `a265af61-7a8d-4adc-81b1-849fedb0b536` — who owns zero stores and has no row in `users` at all.** They were created 2026-08-01 and 2026-08-14, in two sittings with the stock movements bulk-stamped at `11:59:11` and `12:04:05`, i.e. **seven weeks before the first real store launched on 2026-09-25**. The contents are a textbook sample catalogue: Panadol Extra, Coartem, Augmentin 625mg, Zyrtec, Norvasc, Glucophage, Emzor Vitamin C, Benylin, Gaviscon, Dettol Antiseptic; categories Analgesics through Antimalarials; one supplier, "Fidson Distributors Ltd".
- **So these are orphaned demo records from a deleted trial account, not stranded business data.** Nothing owns them, no device can see them, and nobody will miss them. **Attributing them would have been actively harmful** — it would inject a fake ten-product catalogue into a real pharmacy's inventory. The command's refusal to guess was right for a better reason than the one it was written for: the correct owner is *no store*, which no derivation could ever have produced.
- **Decision: leave them.** They are invisible by definition and cost nothing. Deleting them is tidier but is a destructive production write for no functional gain; if it is ever wanted it belongs in its own deliberate cleanup with its own dry run, never folded into a sync repair.
- **Where they most likely came from:** the owner keeps a store (`Pikachuu Stores`) for testing against production, so a deleted earlier test account is the natural explanation for a sample catalogue owned by a user id with no `users` row. That also explains why no derivation could place them: the correct owner is a store that no longer exists.
- **The multi-store-owner theory was wrong.** This entry previously blamed Adaeze owning two stores. The real cause is a *missing* user, not an ambiguous one. Recorded because the wrong explanation is the kind a future reader would otherwise rediscover and act on.
- **Blast radius still unmeasured:** the counts need `SELECT COUNT(*) WHERE store_id IS NULL` per table against production, which §12 forbids without clearance. `sync:backfill-store-ids` with no flags is exactly that measurement and writes nothing; run it before deciding whether any of this matters.
- **What genuinely stays open, and it is narrow:** `requested_products` has no owner column (no `user_id`, no store-scoped FK), so if a real row ever lands there with a NULL `store_id` no derivation can attribute it. **Zero such rows exist today** — the production run reported none. Fixing it needs a signal that does not exist yet (stamp `created_by` going forward, then attribute by that). Until a row appears, this is a latent gap, not an active one.
- **To close entirely:** nothing further on the data. Close this once `requested_products` either gains an owner column or is confirmed never to receive a store-scoped row. The mechanism, the production measurement and the decision above are the whole story.

### A-162 — investigation lapsed: which store id those five stuck `permission_groups` payloads carried is now unknowable (mechanism fixed as A-163)
- **Closed:** 2026-10-20 as **investigation lapsed — no action taken, and none now possible.** The mechanism this was narrowing is fixed: A-163 closed `clearDatabaseForNewStore()`'s orphan path (A-161's fix, commit `b583703d`, 2026-10-06), which shipped weeks ago. The forensic evidence this entry asked for was the `store_id` inside five frozen `_sync_queue` payloads on one iPhone, and that fix drops them — so the rows are gone and the production queries listed below are now unanswerable. It is kept as the record of what was established, not as a success.
- **The one durable suggestion, worth taking whenever that code is next touched:** include the payload's `store_id` and the acting user id in `recordSyncFailure()`'s `logCrash()` context (it sends only `area`/`table`/`recordId`), so the next occurrence of this class arrives self-diagnosing instead of needing this much archaeology.
- **Status:** the *mechanism* by which a row can be queued for one store and pushed under another identity is now fixed — see A-163 in `docs/FIXED_BUGS.md` (`clearDatabaseForNewStore()` left `permission_groups` behind on an account/store switch, and `requeueOrphanedRows()` then re-queued it under the new account). What remains open is only the forensic question of whether that is what actually happened to **this** device, which hinges on one datum nobody has yet: the `store_id` inside those five frozen payloads.
- **Location:** `SyncController::normalizePushPayload()`'s `$allowedStoreIds` guard (`laravel-server`), whose allowed set comes from `resolveOwnershipIdentity()`; the rows themselves come from `ensurePermissionGroupsSeeded()` (`client/lib/db/queries/permission-groups.ts`).
- **What is now established** (beyond A-161's writeup, which this corrects on two points):
  - **The rows were seeded on 2026-09-27**, in a one-day window. `982e8e4b` (2026-09-27) *introduced* the seeding and used `generateId()`; `deterministicDefaultGroupId()` landed later the same day in `a5f40463`. A-161 read the ids' randomness as "a release before 982e8e4b" — before that commit there was no seeding at all, and its deterministic-id check could not have matched by construction.
  - **They were refused on their first push attempt, always.** The guard dates to `132dee87` (2026-09-19), i.e. before the rows existed. There is no regression window and nothing ever accepted them.
  - **The ids are not the deterministic ids of either store this owner really has** (`571582a9…`, and `68e4c0e5…` created 2026-10-05) — consistent with a `generateId()` build, so this says nothing about the store id either way.
- **What the 2026-10-06 prod snapshot ruled out, and what it cannot:** both `user_devices` rows and both `stores` rows belong to one `user_id` (`01a0d876…`), so a second *present-day* account is out. It does **not** speak to 2026-09-27: `user_devices` was created by migration `2026_10_02_000005`, so it holds no history before 2026-10-02, and `UserDeviceTracker::touch()` keys on `(user_id, device_id)` — an earlier account on this same device would only appear as its own row if it had synced since 2026-10-02. A stale-authorization theory is also out: `resolveOwnershipIdentity()` runs `Store::where('user_id', …)` live on every push, not from a cached claim.
- **The strongest remaining candidate, which is cheap to check and was not considered before:** `stores` has used `SoftDeletes` since `2026_09_29_000001_add_soft_deletes_to_stores_table`, and `resolveOwnershipIdentity()`'s `Store::where('user_id', …)` therefore **silently excludes a soft-deleted store**. A third store created and seeded around 2026-09-27 and later soft-deleted (admin panel, or the owner's own store deletion) would produce exactly this refusal forever, with no account switch and no local-only id involved — and a plain `SELECT id, name, user_id, created_at FROM stores` that doesn't ask for `deleted_at` reads as if that store never existed. If this is what happened, the right fix is a decision about rows queued for a store that is gone (drop them client-side, or let the server accept-and-discard), not a tenancy change.
- **The next query to run (read-only), in this order:**
  1. `SELECT id, store_id, name, based_on_role, created_at, updated_at FROM permission_groups WHERE id IN ('392fece4-28a9-4220-bfbf-76cdee14548a','2cc8cdbf-415e-407b-90d8-678c6f6f9de5','16a6f579-b6f5-4fed-b903-bbf8d6c98a73','662ca60f-d088-4e7b-a645-64186d7f1c95','23289841-67ab-487d-9562-4abf88b1a223');` — the five real record ids from Sentry. Expected empty (they were always refused), but if any row exists its `store_id` answers the whole question outright.
  2. `SELECT id, name, user_id, created_at, deleted_at, deleted_by_id, deletion_reason FROM stores WHERE user_id = '01a0d876-79c1-73d9-98b0-7df448f034e8';` — run as raw SQL so soft-deleted rows are included. A third store here, especially one created on or before 2026-09-27 with a non-null `deleted_at`, confirms the soft-delete mechanism above.
  3. Only if both come back empty: `SELECT id, email, role, store_id, created_at FROM users WHERE store_id IN ('571582a9-bc11-43e2-81e9-57999db50294','68e4c0e5-2354-4ff9-98e4-69d3b4c482fa') OR email = 'ibidaadaeze@gmail.com';` — checks for a second `users` row for this person (a staff/PIN row distinct from the owner row) that could have been `$currentUser` on the failing push and hit `resolveOwnershipIdentity()`'s narrow `users.store_id` fallback branch.
- **Also still true, and the reason this class exists at all:** `_sync_queue` is device-global and carries no identity, while push authorization is per-session, so a row queued under one identity is pushed under whatever identity is signed in later. That is deliberate — push is not narrowed by `X-Store-Id`, so one request legitimately carries rows for every store a multi-store owner has — which is why there is no cheap client-side identity filter to add, and why A-163 closes the *orphan* path instead.
- **Worth doing regardless of the answer:** include the payload's `store_id` and the acting user id in `recordSyncFailure()`'s `logCrash()` context (it currently sends only `area`/`table`/`recordId`), so the next occurrence of this class arrives self-diagnosing instead of needing this much archaeology. Note also that A-161's fix (committed on `dev` as `b583703d`) had not reached production when these events fired at 08:38 on 2026-10-06, so those five rows are probably **still** in that iPhone's `_sync_queue` until that deploy lands — reading it directly is the one way to get the payload without any of the queries above, and the window for doing so closes once the fix ships and drops them.

### A-266 — a customer desktop keeps losing its date, and the hard clock lock is the accepted design rather than a defect
- **Closed:** 2026-10-20 as a **design-decision record, not a defect.** The hard clock lock is correct against the adversary it exists for — the store owner is the party backdating checks are meant to stop, so any offline escape is an escape for them too — and the bounded degraded mode is **deliberately not built**, because rows written under an untrusted clock are exactly the rows that get the wrong trading day and lose every `stale_timestamp` conflict. Revisit only if a till reports being locked out and unable to clear it.
- **Nothing in it is a code action any more.** The lock-screen work shipped 2026-10-14 and is pinned by `client/__tests__/license-blocked-card-clock-cause.test.tsx`; the remaining two steps are physical — ask the beta pharmacy whether they have been locked out, and replace the CR2032 and check `w32time` on that machine.
- **Found:** 2026-10-14, reported by the founder from the field. **Not measured in the repo, which is why it is written down here** — a review session dismissed it as hypothetical on the grounds that the only clock evidence in the codebase is A-247's ~8-hours-fast till. Both are real: the fast till is the measured one, this is the observed one.
- **What is observed:** a specific customer PC repeatedly loses its date and time. The classic cause is a dead CMOS battery (CR2032), which resets the clock to a BIOS default on power-off — and a Nigerian pharmacy takes several power cuts a week, so the reset recurs until the part is replaced. Unconfirmed: whether it is the battery, a BIOS clock reset by switching off at the wall, or something else. Nobody has opened the machine.
- **Why it matters beyond the clock:** the licence guard keeps a `last_monotonic_time` watermark and locks the till when wall time reads below it (`client/lib/licensing/licensing-manager.ts`). A battery reset is always a backwards jump, so each reset locks that till. The way out is to correct the clock and verify against the server, which needs connectivity **at that moment** — during a power cut, in a shop, that is not guaranteed. The failure mode is "cannot sell until the internet comes back", and for a pharmacy still half on paper, every lock is an argument for the paper.
- **What already exists, so this is not A-191 again:** the lock clears without a support call, though **not on its own** — `license-guard.tsx` reaches `reconcileClockWithServer()` only from `performCheck({ refreshFromCloud: true })`, i.e. when someone presses **Check Again** while online, and it resets the watermark to server time once the clock agrees. Nothing runs automatically on mount, so the lock screen has to tell the operator both steps. And `overrideClockLockout()` gives support a supervised way in, authorised by a live `admin_till_sessions` row rather than anything forgeable on the device. A-191 (the permanent lockout, where a fast clock wrote the future into the watermark and correcting it read as a rollback) is **fixed**; see `docs/FIXED_BUGS.md` and `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-08-license-clock-recovery-design.md`.
- **The decision, stated so it is not silently re-made:** the hard lock is correct against the adversary — the store owner is the party backdating checks exist to stop, so any offline escape is an escape for them too. It is wrong only for the honest user with no connectivity at that moment. A bounded degraded mode (sell, flagged `clock_untrusted`, licence countdown frozen at its last trusted value, hard lock after N sales or 48 floor-hours) is the known alternative, and it is **deliberately not built**: rows written under an untrusted clock are precisely the rows that get the wrong trading day and lose every `stale_timestamp` conflict, so it trades a loud failure for a silent one. Revisit if a till reports being locked out and unable to clear it.
- **Next steps, in order:** (1) **ask the beta pharmacy directly** whether they have been locked out, how often, and how long it took to clear — do not wait for the complaint to arrive; (2) ~~write the lock screen for this cause~~ **done 2026-10-14**: the clock-lock card now names the CR2032 and gives the two steps out, pinned by `client/__tests__/license-blocked-card-clock-cause.test.tsx`; (3) replace the battery and check `w32time` on that machine.

### A-290 — the store-details Sync tab had no client permission gate
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, `web/` only, TDD. Full reasoning in `web/AGENTS.md`, "The Sync tab is gated on `view_platform_health` (A-290)".
- **Was:** all five panels of the tab are server-gated on `view_platform_health` (`routes/api.php`), but `StoreDetailTabs` gated nothing. A `platform_admin` or `agent` without that permission saw a **Sync** tab and landed on five cards each rendering its own "unavailable" state from a 403. The panel-level *actions* were already gated, so the convention and the helper both existed; only the container was never gated.
- **Now:** `checkHasPermission(user, "view_platform_health")` hides the `TabsTrigger` and the `TabsContent`, `resolveTab()` takes the same flag so a shared `?tab=sync` link resolves to `overview`, and `StoreSyncTab` repeats the check itself so it does not depend on its caller (A-135's lesson). Hide, never disable, matching the three action components beside it.
- **The badge is suppressed rather than degraded.** `syncVerdict()` would have returned `Unavailable` off the two 403s — honest, but an alarm on a tab nobody can open, and two 403s on every store page view. The two hooks are passed `enabled: canViewSync` instead, so the verdict is `null` and the existing "no pill, empty live region" path renders.
- **No browser smoke test was possible here (§9), so the clicks are written down** in the `AGENTS.md` section above: an operator without the permission must see a two-tab strip, a `?tab=sync` deep link must open Overview, and the network tab must show no `table-agreement`/`stock-divergence` request; granting the permission must bring all five panels back.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** client-side only and strictly subtractive — the server already refused these calls, so nothing that used to work stops working.
- **Coverage:** `web/__tests__/admin-store-sync-tab-gating.test.tsx` — 6 cases, 4 RED before (no trigger, no content, the `?tab=sync` fallback, the two queries staying disabled, and `StoreSyncTab`'s own guard). The two permitted cases were green before and exist to pin that the gate is not inverted. `admin-store-detail-tabs.test.tsx` gained an auth-store mock: it had none, so every one of its Sync assertions would otherwise have been passing on an unauthenticated user.

### A-305 — the pull watermark was shared across stores, so a multi-store device missed everything the other store changed while it was away
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, client only, TDD. **The actual cause of the data loss investigated this session**, which had been attributed to a pull cursor drifting ahead of rows it never received. That was the symptom.
- **Was:** `_sync_state` was `PRIMARY KEY (table_name)` with no store column, and `switchStore()` flips the resolver, mirrors localStorage and clears the React Query cache but never touches it (`clearDatabaseForNewStore()` does wipe it, but runs only from the onboarding "link a different cloud store" flow). The pull sends `last_synced_at` per **table**, so a device that spent a week on store A advanced every watermark to "now"; on switching to B the pull asked for B's rows changed since *now*, and **everything B changed that week was never delivered**. Every watermark read perfectly current throughout, which is why nothing looked wrong.
- **Measured on production:** `DRX-8DY6G5XH8`, used for both Nest Amawbia and Enugu Agidi daily, reported on Amawbia `sales 87 vs 104`, `sale_items 2,207 vs 2,725`, `sale_item_batches 2,219 vs 2,739`, `stock_movements 5,399 vs 5,919` — **unchanged across nine syncs over three hours**, with `_sync_state` for every one of them current. Its stock also read **+1,671 units / +₦507,300** over cloud, consistent with 520 undelivered sale movements whose decrements were never applied. A Force Full Resync fixed it instantly, which is exactly what clearing `_sync_state` does.
- **Now:** `PRIMARY KEY (store_id, table_name)`, and every read and write takes the **run's** store — which is only possible because A-304 gave a run one identity. That covers `pull.ts`'s two progress upserts and its state read, and all five of `pull-window.ts`'s statements (rewind, restore, stamp, the never-pulled drop, and the stash/restore pair `forceFullResync()` uses, which is now scoped so an escape hatch pulled for one store leaves the other's windows intact).
- **The migration rebuilds the table, because SQLite cannot alter a primary key.** `scopeSyncStateToStore()` drops any scratch table, creates the scoped one, copies every row, drops the old one and renames — the same shape as `relaxPurchaseOrdersSupplierIdNullable()`. It is gated on the column's absence, so it is idempotent, and wrapped in try/catch: on failure the original table is left in place and the device behaves exactly as it does today.
- **`DROP TABLE IF EXISTS _sync_state_scoped` before the CREATE is the load-bearing line, added on review.** The rebuild is four statements with no transaction, so a process death between the copy and the drop leaves a **populated** scratch table. Without that first drop, every later boot's copy hits the composite primary key, throws, is swallowed by the catch — and because every pull statement now names `store_id` against a table that still lacks it, the device's pulls fail on a missing column **for ever, logged only to `console.error`**. Pushes keep working, so the till looks alive while it silently stops receiving anything. Low probability, permanent consequence; `__tests__/init-database-migrations.test.ts` pins it with a pre-populated scratch table and that case fails without the line.
- **Other orderings are all benign, checked one by one on review.** A death after the DROP but before the RENAME loses the watermarks and costs one full re-pull — `SCHEMA_SQL`'s `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` runs before migrations on both backends and recreates the table in the new shape. On the sql.js path the adapter's `run()` does not mark the database dirty, so nothing persists until the next real write and an interrupted attempt simply reruns from the untouched export; `saveDatabase()` exports the whole database, so there is no partial-persist state to land in.
- **Existing rows are stamped with the device's store only when it has exactly one.** That is the common case and it re-pulls nothing. A device with no store, or more than one, keeps its rows under `''`, which matches no real store — so the first pull per store starts from scratch. Deliberate: a watermark whose store cannot be known must not be claimed for a store, and `backfillStoreIdOnLegacyRows()` already sets that precedent by refusing multi-store devices. The cost is one full pull per store on such a device, once.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** client-only; the server already takes whatever timestamps it is given. An un-migrated device behaves as today, so the exposure window is a single app start. The `stores` table's own window is keyed the same way even though it is not per-tenant data — consistency beats a special case, and `syncSubscriptionStatus()` resolves its own run for it.
- **Coverage:** `__tests__/pull-watermark-is-per-store.test.ts` — 3 cases (a pull for B is not handed A's watermark; a store still uses its own; syncing B leaves A's untouched), all RED on the old schema. Plus one case in `__tests__/init-database-migrations.test.ts` pinning the rebuild: a table-keyed `_sync_state` with a real watermark and `rewind_count` is carried over stamped with the sole store, and two stores can then hold separate watermarks for the same table — which the old key made impossible. Full client suite **3,016 passed**; `test:schema` clean (`_sync_state` is client-only, so it is not compared).
- **Four more `_sync_state` readers were missed in the first commit and fixed in the follow-up**, found when the owner asked whether the sweep had been exhaustive — it had not. `movementLogIsComplete()` (`stock-auto-heal.ts`) and the mid-window refusal in `stock-integrity.ts` both read `WHERE table_name = 'stock_movements'` with no store and took the first row: on a migrated two-store device the `''` row sorts first, so both would have read "log complete" regardless of the active store's real state — **in the code path that rebuilds stock quantities**. `diagnostics.ts`'s device-report listing showed every store's rows with no way to tell them apart, in the instrument this whole investigation depended on. `retention.ts`'s `MAX(last_synced_at)` is correct unscoped and now says so in a comment: any store's server timestamp bounds real time, and the audit-log prune is device-wide. All three scoped reads use the ambient store deliberately — none runs inside a sync run — and `stock-auto-heal.ts` joins the store-identity allowlist with that reason.
- **Two notes for whoever reads this next.** `SELECT id FROM stores` counts soft-deleted rows, so a device that once held a since-deleted store is treated as multi-store and does one unnecessary full re-pull; harmless, and not worth the fragility of a column-existence check. And `relaxPurchaseOrdersSupplierIdNullable()` has the same un-guarded-scratch-table flaw this entry fixes, one statement earlier and with a milder consequence — filed as A-306.
- **One harness correction worth noting:** `push-never-rewinds-for-telemetry.test.ts` failed until its fixtures named the store. Its `_sync_state` rows were written without one while the harness had `store-1` active — the same class of mistake as the bug itself, caught by the suite rather than by reading.

### A-304 — a sync run resolved its store twice, from two sources, and mislabelled what it reported
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, client only, TDD. Raised by the owner asking whether A-301's fix handled the store-switch class *properly* or merely narrowed it. It had narrowed it: the write side was correct, the attribution side was not.
- **Was:** a run had no store identity. `client.ts` stamped `X-Store-Id` from `getStoredActiveStoreId()` (localStorage) **at request time**, while the three report builders read the module resolver **once at the start** of the run. A run then spends seconds or minutes on batches and pages, and `switchStore()` flips both sources without cancelling an in-flight `runSync()` — so a run that computed its digests for store A could have them filed against store B.
- **What it cost:** on 2026-10-19 a device reported Agidi's digest against Amawbia's cloud and the panel read `-4,129 units / -₦6,243,954` and "13 tables disagree" on a device that was in step. No data moved; roughly twenty minutes went into chasing a catastrophe that did not exist, and the wrong conclusion ("her local data is mixed") was drawn and acted on before the figures were checked.
- **Now:** `runSync()` mints `{ id, storeId }` via `resolveSyncRun()` (`sync-engine/sync-run.ts`) and threads it through `pushChanges`/`pullChanges` → `apiClient`, which takes the store as an explicit `runStoreId` and prefers it over the ambient one. `buildStockFingerprint`, `buildTableFingerprint` and `buildQueueStateReport` take the store as a **required argument** — the compiler then found every call site, which is the guard working rather than anyone remembering.
- **`sync-run.ts` is the single file permitted to read ambient store state**, pinned by `__tests__/sync-engine-store-identity.test.ts`: it fails if any other sync-engine file imports `getActiveStoreId`/`getStoredActiveStoreId`. Verified in both directions — green as written, RED when that import was deliberately reintroduced into a fingerprint builder.
- **Four of the originally-filed six sites were not bugs**, and the entry said so before shipping: `health-check.ts` and `reconcile-quantities.ts` resolve the store and issue their request in the same tick with no `await` between (and the server scopes the latter by each batch's product's store anyway), `device-report-command.ts` uses it for a prose label, `stock-integrity.ts` for local writes only. They are allowlisted with those reasons written into the test, along with the guard's limit: the allowlist is per **file**, so a genuinely run-scoped ambient read added to one of those five would not be caught.
- **Deliberately not built: cancel-on-switch, and the server-side mismatch check.** A run-scoped identity makes a late-landing response correctly labelled, so cancellation is an optimisation rather than a correctness requirement — and a cancellation rule has to be remembered at every new call site, which is the failure mode this whole class comes from. The server half would catch nothing in the field: an old bundle sends no store in its fingerprint payload, and a new one builds payload and header from the same `run.storeId`, so only a future client regression could diverge — which the structural test catches at CI time instead of silently.
- **Deviation from the review, stated because it is a weaker guarantee.** The review wanted the run parameter *required* on `pushChanges`/`pullChanges`. Thirty-seven test files call those directly, so the parameter stays optional and `sync-run.ts` resolves it at the entry point when absent. The invariant (one resolution per run) is identical; the enforcement is a structural test rather than the compiler.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** client-only, no contract change. The server already accepts whatever `X-Store-Id` it is given, so a run-frozen header is indistinguishable from today's to every server version. Nothing to retire.
- **Coverage:** `__tests__/sync-run-store-identity.test.ts` (2 cases) and `__tests__/sync-engine-store-identity.test.ts` (1). Full client suite **3,012 passed**.
- **The behavioural test passed against the broken code on its first attempt**, and that is worth recording: it flipped only the module resolver, while the header reads localStorage. `switchStore()` sets both; a test that moves one proves nothing. Corrected, then confirmed RED against the old `push.ts` (`undefined` rather than `store-a` — no store was passed at all). It also pins the argument handed to the API client, not `syncHeaders()`'s assembly of it, because the client is mocked; that limit is written in the file.

### A-302 — the server accepted a product naming another store's category; it now heals it on ingest
- **Superseded in part by A-321 (2026-10-23):** the guard now also covers `supplier_id` on `purchase_orders`, `supplier_payments` and `stock_batches`; on UPDATE it judges the store being written (`$payload['store_id'] ?? $model->store_id`), and a batch's store is its product's; a soft-deleted referent is re-owned too; and a supplier is only re-owned between one owner's stores. Categories keep this entry's behaviour, including across tenants. Where a bullet below says otherwise, A-321's entry is current.
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, server only, TDD. The other half of A-301: that fix stops devices *creating* the state, this one stops the server *storing* it — which is what protects the data while pre-A-301 bundles are still in the field.
- **A-189 is the originating report of this class** (5,088 cross-wired products, 2026-10-08) and is now closed — see its entry above.
- **Was:** nothing checked that a `products.category_id` belonged to the product's own store. All 4,638 mis-stamped products A-301 measured were accepted by this path, and because every cloud-side read joins `categories` without regard to ownership, nothing on the server or in the admin panel ever showed it — only a store-scoped device did, as "Uncategorized".
- **Now:** `App\Services\Sync\CrossStoreReferenceGuard` **remaps** the reference onto the pushing store's own same-named category, creating or reviving it if absent, by calling the same `CrossTenantCategoryRepairService::resolveOwnedCategoryId()` the production repair command uses. The server therefore heals on ingest, and the repair no longer has to be re-run by hand after each old-bundle burst.
- **Remap, not drop, and the first version of this entry had it wrong.** Dropping the key would store a permanent `NULL` on an INSERT — a reference no device will ever push again, which is A-294's own failure shape — and on an UPDATE would discard a legitimate re-categorisation. The pushed id is wrong but the **name** is the user's intent: A-301's 670 re-categorisations were all to "drugs", "cosmetics", "provision"; the bug only swapped *which store's*.
- **Not in `normalizePushPayload()`, also corrected.** For an UPDATE that normaliser *unsets* `store_id` when the payload carries none, so the row's own store is not in the payload at all. The guard runs in each operation branch instead, with the row's resolved store — `$payload['store_id']` for an INSERT, `$model->store_id` for an UPDATE, never the request's `$currentStoreId` (an owner's header-less push resolves to their first store, which A-301 showed is not the row's store).
- **Three cases deliberately untouched, each pinned by a test:** a referent that exists nowhere (legitimate offline ordering — A-294's case), a referent whose own `store_id` is `NULL` (A-288/A-303), and a category created earlier in the same batch (already correct, because the guard runs after the in-request `$idMap` substitution). No pre-pass over the batch's distinct ids, for exactly that reason: lookups must be lazy and post-remap.
- **Recorded as an event, not a refusal.** `cross_store_reference` is written to `sync_failures` so an operator can watch old bundles still producing the state, but it is never appended to the response's `failed` and is counted in **neither** `changes_refused` nor `changes_conflicted` — the change was accepted. A-294's planned detector could not have counted this: it counts dangling *state*, and a remap leaves none.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** additive. Request and response shapes are unchanged, so no old bundle sees a new slug or parks a row. An old bundle that still produces the A-301 state gets a 200, its row lands with the right category, and its local copy corrects on the next pull. Permanent integrity rule, not a shim — the server can never prove no old bundle remains — so there is no retirement condition.
- **Scope is `products.category_id` only, on purpose.** Cross-store `supplier_id` is possible (the client remap covered suppliers too) but has **never been measured** — A-294 measured *absent* supplier referents, a different query. Measure it read-only first; `stock_batches` also needs a second join, since it is not in `STORE_ID_BACKFILL_TABLES` and takes its store from its product.
- **Cost:** two memos per request (referenced id → store/name, and store|name → owned id), so at most one read per distinct id in a 50-change batch. No `lockForUpdate` on the category read.
- **Coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPushCrossStoreCategoryTest.php` — 6 cases, 3 RED before (INSERT remap, UPDATE remap, category created for a store that lacked the name). Plus one `SyncFailureRecorderTest` case pinning that the new reason moves neither tally. Full suite 1,471 passed.
- **One test correction worth recording:** the "exists nowhere" case first asserted the stored `category_id` and failed — SQLite enforces the `products.category_id` foreign key that production does not declare at all (A-292), so the row never landed and the assertion was comparing against a missing row. It now asserts the guard's own record instead, with that reason inline. The same trap as A-297's test; it will catch the next person too.

### A-301 — a two-store device repointed one store's entire catalogue at the other store's categories
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, client only, two store filters, TDD. The worst defect found this month, and the answer to a complaint that had been open for three weeks.
- **A-189 is the originating report of this class** (5,088 cross-wired products, 2026-10-08); its repair is the command run below, and it is now closed — see its entry above.
- **The complaint it explains:** a store owner reported that "most products I reassigned categories for have reverted to uncategorized", repeatedly, over three weeks — 670 category edits logged in `activity_logs` since 28/09, the affected products sitting at `_version 11` from being redone by hand again and again. Nothing was reverting. Her edits were landing and then being overwritten with *another store's* category ids, which no device of her store can resolve.
- **Measured on production, 2026-10-19:** `sync:repair-cross-tenant-categories` reported **2,138** products of store `571582a9` (Nest Amawbia) pointing at store `68e4c0e5`'s (Enugu Agidi) categories, and **2,500** the other way — across the same 11 names (`beverages, busicuit, cosmetics, drugs, machine, perfume, provision, soap, sweet, toiletries, wine`), with **0 categories created**, i.e. both stores already owned same-named rows. `products.updated_at` dates the whole Amawbia side to one four-minute burst: **14/10 08:39 (1,099), 08:40 (1,030), 08:43 (9)**. Both directions have been repaired with `--apply`.
- **Was, and it is two unscoped queries:**
  1. `pull.ts`'s duplicate-name reconciliation selected `SELECT id, name FROM categories WHERE (_deleted = 0 OR _deleted IS NULL)` — **every** local row, whatever store it belonged to. The pulled page only ever holds the **active** store's categories, so another store's local category is never in `serverIds`, looks orphaned, and is matched by name.
  2. `remapForeignKey()`'s `UPDATE products SET category_id = ?, _synced = 0 WHERE category_id = ?` was **also** unscoped, so the match above repointed *that store's whole catalogue*. The reconciliation then soft-deleted the losing store's category row locally, and the rewritten products — now `_synced = 0` — were queued, pushed, and **accepted**, because the server does not check that a product's category belongs to its store (A-294).
- **Why it looked like nothing was wrong:** the admin panel and every cloud-side read join `categories` without regard to ownership, so the figures resolve perfectly. Only a *device* is store-scoped, so only a till shows the damage — as "Uncategorized", for ever, on the losing store. A single-store till is immune, which is why one device in the same store was always fine.
- **One burst per store switch, not continuous.** The `updated_at` clustering shows this fires when a device reconciles the newly-active store's categories, then goes quiet. On a laptop used for two stores daily that is a fresh burst a day, which is why every previous run of the repair appeared to "not work".
- **Now: the scope comes from the data, never from the ambient active store.** `pull.ts` pairs each local row against the **pulled page's** own `store_id` set and passes that row's `store_id` to `remapForeignKey()`, which takes it as an explicit parameter and applies `AND (store_id = ? OR store_id IS NULL)` per ref table (guarded by `columnExists`). `push.ts`'s `id_map` branch reads the losing row's `store_id` and passes it the same way. `NULL` stays in scope deliberately: a legacy row with no store is the pre-multi-tenancy case this remap exists for.
- **The first version of this fix scoped by `getActiveStoreId()`, and review rejected it.** That is a *different source* from the one that scoped the request — `client.ts` stamps `X-Store-Id` from `getStoredActiveStoreId()` (localStorage) while the reconciliation read the module resolver. `switchStore()` flips the resolver synchronously but does **not** cancel an in-flight `runSync()`, so a pull requested for store A whose response lands after a switch to B would be reconciled with B's categories as the candidates — repointing B's catalogue at A's category, the identical corruption, on exactly the device that had already suffered it twice. The same hole existed on the push side: an `id_map` remap scoped to the active store misses the losing store's products *and* tombstones its category, leaving "Uncategorized" with no path back. Scoping by the losing row's own store removes every timing dependency. `client/__tests__/duplicate-name-reconciliation.test.ts` pins it with a resolver/page mismatch case that fails against that first version.
- **`columnExists` is not a silent no-fix path:** `runSyncColumnMigrations()` adds `store_id` to `products`/`categories` during `initDatabase()`, before any pull can run, so the guard only ever evaluates false in the test harness. That is true of the **column**; a legacy `NULL`-store *row* on a multi-store device is a separate, still-open case — A-303.
- **`serverIdByName` is keyed by store **and** name, not name alone.** `client.ts` only sends `X-Store-Id` when a stored active store exists, and an owner's is set by the switcher — so a header-less pull returns every store that owner holds (`SyncController::applyPullTenantScope()` falls back to `$ownedStoreIds`). On such a page a bare name matched whichever store's row appeared last, and the remap — correctly scoped to the losing row's store — repointed that store's products at **another** store's category. Same symptom, different door; closed in the same change rather than filed, because it is the natural completion of "take the scope from the data".
- **Fail-closed on a page with no store ids, deliberately.** If a page carried no `store_id` at all, every local row with a store is skipped and nothing reconciles. That cannot happen for a POS client — `applyPullTenantScope()` scopes both tables with `whereIn('store_id', …)`, `NULL` never matches `whereIn`, and the only unscoped path is a `super_admin` bearer, which is not a client identity. A permissive fallback was considered and rejected: it would re-enable the unscoped rewrite in precisely the ambiguous case.
- **§8 while here:** both `DUPLICATE_NAME_TABLES[table]` lookups are now behind `Object.hasOwn`. The table name comes from a server response, and a key of `"constructor"` resolved to `Object` — truthy — and then interpolated into SQL. The server is trusted, so this was a latent nit rather than a hole, but §8 forbids the shape outright.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** client-only, no contract touched. But **an old bundle in the field keeps doing the damage until it updates**, and the server still accepts the result — so whether `A-294`'s proposed detector should become a push-time *refusal* is the open question, raised in review rather than decided here.
- **This fix stops the bleeding; it does not heal a device already damaged.** On the two-store device the losing store's categories are `_deleted = 1` **locally** via a raw `UPDATE` with no queue entry, and the server's row never changed — so an incremental pull never re-delivers them and that store keeps rendering "Uncategorized" after the deploy. The pull's upsert does overwrite `_deleted` from the server, so a **Force Full Resync (or a `categories` pull-window rewind) on that device, per store, is a required deployment step**, not an afterthought.
- **Coverage:** `client/__tests__/duplicate-name-reconciliation.test.ts` — two new cases, a device holding two stores that both have a "Drugs" category, pulling one store's. It asserts the active store's own collision still reconciles, the other store's product keeps its category, and the other store's category is **not** soft-deleted. Verified RED on the old code by stashing both source files: it rewrote the other store's product and deleted its category, the production burst in miniature. Full client suite 3,008 passed.
- **The test `ALTER TABLE`s `store_id` onto `categories`/`products`/`suppliers`** because `SCHEMA_SQL` does not carry it — modelling a migrated device, called out inline so the next reader does not take it for a shortcut. It runs in `beforeAll` **before any pull**, because `getValidColumns()` caches per table and a pull that warmed that cache first would land its rows with a `NULL` `store_id` and quietly stop the new cases from testing anything. The resolver is reset in `afterEach`, not at the end of a test body, so a failing assertion cannot leak it into the next case.

### A-295 — one name collision re-queued every unsynced row on the device, for ever
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, client only, TDD. Driver identified on review after the first diagnosis was wrong — see "What the first diagnosis got wrong" below, because the wrong version was committed before it was checked.
- **Measured on production (store `571582a9`, Nest Amawbia, `sync_failures` 08/10–16/10):** `products · version_conflict · INSERT` **9,181** refusals, `sale_item_batches` **706**, against a 2,735-product catalogue. A `LEFT JOIN` from `sync_failures.record_id` to each table returns **`missing_in_cloud = 0` for every one** — 9,181 of 9,181 and 706 of 706 already in the cloud. Roughly 3.4 whole-table sweeps' worth.
- **Was, and the loop is the point:**
  1. A `categories`/`suppliers` name collision resolves via `id_map`, and the client calls `remapForeignKey()` to repoint its local rows.
  2. That function flipped the remapped rows to `_synced = 0`, then called `requeueOrphanedRows()` **on the whole referenced table** — `['products']` for a category — because `_synced = 0` alone never reaches `push()`, which reads only from `_sync_queue`. One collision therefore re-queued **every** unsynced product on the device, not the handful it had touched.
  3. Each swept row goes up as an `INSERT` carrying the local `_version`. `SyncController::push()` treats an INSERT for a row it already holds as a version-checked UPDATE.
  4. That store's devices could not converge on `products` (`sync-pull-window · rewind budget exhausted for products … this device cannot converge on its own`), so their versions were stale and every one was refused.
  5. A `version_conflict` is terminal client-side: `push.ts` drops the queue row and leaves `_synced = 0` — so the row is eligible for the next collision's sweep. Self-sustaining.
- **Now:** `remapForeignKey()` records the ids it actually remapped (a `SELECT id WHERE column = oldId` before the UPDATE) and passes them to `requeueOrphanedRows(tables, onlyIds)`, whose new optional per-table id filter is the whole mechanism. A remap that touched nothing queues nothing; a remap that touched one product queues one product.
- **The "settle `_synced` on a refusal" idea was proposed, reviewed and rejected (2026-10-19) — because the useful half already shipped server-side on 2026-10-17.** `SyncController::push()` probes for an existing row (`:352-360`), converts a resent INSERT to an UPDATE with `$isResentInsert = true`, and on a version mismatch calls `reopenPullWindow()`, returns the server's `_version` in `versions`, and **never adds it to `$failed`** (`:624-645`, commits `81942b1d` + `7143730f`). The client then treats it as a success, so `markSynced()` sets `_synced = 1` and applies the server version. A resent INSERT therefore already self-settles, with no new reason string in the contract (§11). The 9,181 refusals measured here are all dated 08/10–16/10, before that change; new rows of this shape dated after the backend deploy would mean production is not running `main`.
- **And flipping `_synced` for a genuine UPDATE conflict would have been actively harmful.** `_synced = 0` with no queue row is the only local marker that a row's content differs from the cloud: the device report's "Unsynced with no queue entry" line reads it (`diagnostics-detail.ts:171-180` — it is how the 4,477-row backlog was found), and `batchCostSnapshot` shields such a batch from the stock-integrity fold (`client/AGENTS.md:376`). Flipping it would have **hidden** the category-edit loss this entry describes, not surfaced it. `client/AGENTS.md:1035-1041` already states the rule: a `version_conflict` stays unsettled because the next pull is what reconciles it. `markConflictSettled()` is for reasons no pull can ever settle, which is the opposite case.
- **Deliberately NOT changed: the terminal-conflict semantics in step 5.** Flipping `_synced = 1` on a refused INSERT would also drain the existing backlog, but `push-version-conflict-handling.test.ts` records the intent that a conflicted row is *not* marked synced, and once the sweep is scoped the loop cannot sustain itself — the only remaining re-queue is `requeueOrphanedRowsOnce()`, once per install. Left alone as a semantics change nothing now needs.
- **The existing backlog is not retro-repaired.** A device already holding rows at `_synced = 0` (the device report's "Unsynced with no queue entry" line — 4,477 `sale_item_batches` and 76 `stock_audits` on `DRX-8DY6G5XH8`) keeps them until its next once-per-install sweep, which will push them once and, if refused, stop. That is the correct cost: bounded, one round, no loop.
- **What the first diagnosis got wrong, recorded because the wrong version shipped in `docs/`:** it blamed a per-boot requeue. `DatabaseProvider.tsx:146-147` calls `requeueOrphanedRowsOnce()`, which is once **per install** behind `dumos_orphan_requeue_v1`, cleared only by `clearDatabaseForNewStore()`. The phrase "re-queued every boot" is a **label in `diagnostics-report.ts:116`**, not a mechanism. It also claimed Force Full Resync re-pushes the backlog; `forceFullResync()` touches `_sync_state` only, never the queue. Both were corrected before the real driver — `remapForeignKey()`'s second, ungated caller of the same sweep — was found.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** client-only, no contract touched. The new parameter is optional and every existing caller keeps the whole-table behaviour.
- **Coverage:** `client/__tests__/remap-requeues-only-remapped-rows.test.ts` — 2 cases, both RED before. The second is the sharper one: a remap matching **no** row still queued an unrelated unsynced product. Full client suite 3,007 passed; `__tests__/requeue-strips-null-columns.test.ts`, `reconcile-identity.test.ts`, `orphan-requeue-runs-once-per-install.test.ts` and `push-id-map-reconciliation.test.ts` pin that the unscoped path still works where it is still used.
- **One new limit, from review:** the scoped query binds one placeholder per remapped row (`id IN (?, ?, …)`) where the old one bound none. SQLite's ceiling is 32,766 (sql.js 1.13 and `@tauri-apps/plugin-sql` 2.x both bundle ≥3.32), so a single category or supplier holding more than ~32k referencing rows would throw inside the push batch transaction and roll that batch back. No catalogue in the field is within two orders of magnitude of that; chunk the ids if one ever is.
- **§3 cleanup on touch:** the eight-line comment block above that call is gone; its content is this entry.
- **One claim in the first version of this entry was wrong:** it implied `reconcileIdentity()` shared this sweep. It does not call `remapForeignKey()` or `requeueOrphanedRows()` at all — it issues its own `UPDATE`s and rewrites existing queue payloads (`reconcile-identity.ts:248-322`). The repair path was never affected by this change.

### A-297 — a child row whose parent resolved to no store was deletable by any tenant
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, server only, one expression plus a method split, TDD. Found in the review of A-296's repair plan, not by a report.
- **Was:** `SyncController::resolveChangeStoreId()` resolved a child table's owner through its parent FK and returned `null` when that yielded nothing. `authorizeChangeTarget()` documents `null` as "fall back to `user_id` ownership, not denied", and its next guard is `if (!isset($model->user_id)) return true;` — so for the six child tables that carry no `user_id` (`stock_batches`, `sale_items`, `sale_item_batches`, `return_items`, `prescription_items`, `purchase_order_items`) a `null` resolution authorized **unconditionally**. Any authenticated device of any tenant could soft-delete such a row by id.
- **Two real shapes reach it, both measured on production:** a parent that is absent entirely (A-296 — 22 orphaned `purchase_order_items` from POs created and deleted seconds apart) and a parent whose own `store_id` is `NULL` (A-288 — three such `purchase_orders`). Not exploited: it needs the row's uuid, and the rows it exposes are the ones no read path can see.
- **Why the fall-open was reasoned about and still wrong:** `authorizeInsertTarget()`'s doc-comment argues the attack "only ever yields parents that do resolve to a real, foreign store". True for INSERT, where an unresolvable parent means there is nothing to attach to. False for DELETE, where the target is a row that already exists and whose owner is simply unknown to the resolver.
- **Now:** `resolveChangeStoreId()` is `resolveParentStoreId(...) ?? ($model->store_id ?? null)` — parent's store, else the row's own `store_id`, else `null`. The parent half moved into `resolveParentStoreId()` so the fallback cannot shadow it. A child's own `store_id` stays non-authoritative for **scoping** (A-288's rule is unchanged); it is consulted only as a second ownership opinion when the parent has none.
- **The fall-open is deliberately kept** for a row with no store on either side: a child pushed before its parent, or a legacy row, must still sync (§11). The fix narrows the hole to exactly the case where the data says who owns the row and the old code ignored it.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** no contract changed — no new field, no payload shape, nothing for a client to understand. The only behavioural change is that a cross-tenant DELETE of an unattributable child row now returns `forbidden` instead of succeeding, which `push.ts` already parks and retries (A-165's semantics).
- **Coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPushOwnershipTest.php` — 2 cases. The first (attacker deletes the victim's line under a NULL-store parent) was RED before: `Failed asserting that actual size 0 matches expected size 1` on `failed`, i.e. the delete was accepted. The second (a line with no store of its own still deletes) was green before and exists to pin that the fall-open survives. **The unresolvable parent is modelled as a NULL-store parent rather than a missing one on purpose:** SQLite enforces the `cascadeOnDelete` FK that production does not declare at all (A-292), so a true orphan cannot be inserted in a test — the test file says so inline, because the obvious reading is that the author simply didn't model the real shape.
- **Full suite:** 1,464 passed, 1 skipped.
- **Bypass closed 2026-10-23 by A-298 (above).** Review found the fix bypassable by a crafted payload: `push()` applies `$payload['store_id']` to a NULL-`store_id` target **before** `authorizeChangeTarget()` runs, so an attacker who names their own store in the payload makes the new fallback resolve to *their* store and passes. Equivalent to the pre-fix fall-open, so this fix is not a regression and is worth keeping — but the hole it was written to close was still reachable that way until A-298 moved the claim after authorization.

### A-289 — the client's sync table name was used as a physical table name in the pull cursor
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, server only, TDD. The agreement-panel half was already fixed on 2026-10-18 by removing `audit_logs` from both sides of the comparison (`docs/DEVICE_TABLE_AGREEMENT.md`); this closes the general confusion the entry stayed open for.
- **Was:** `SyncController::applyPullCursor()` probed `Schema::hasColumn($table, '_synced_at')` with `$table` being the **client's** sync table name. `getModelForTable()` maps `audit_logs` onto `ActivityLog`, which has no `$table` override, so the pull reads and writes the physical `activity_logs` while the probe asked the unrelated legacy physical `audit_logs` table.
- **No device is affected today, and that is worth stating plainly.** Both tables happen to carry `_synced_at` — `activity_logs` from `add_synced_at_to_remaining_tables`, `audit_logs` from `align_schema_with_client_db`, which created it with the full `_version`/`_synced`/`_synced_at`/`_deleted` set — so the probe returned `true` either way and the cursor was correct by coincidence. **The behaviour of this fix is identical.** What it removes is the coincidence: had the legacy table ever been dropped, or had either table's `_synced_at` differed, every activity-log row whose `updated_at` did not move would have been skipped by the incremental pull for ever, invisibly.
- **Now:** the probe reads `$query->getModel()->getTable()`, which is by construction the physical table the pull queries, and the per-request memo is keyed by that name too. No second `getModelForTable()` call: the query handed in was already built from the resolved model.
- **Sweep, which the fix's brief asked for.** Every other dynamic table name in `app/` resolves correctly already: `stampSyncedAt()` and `reopenPullWindow()` both read `$model->getTable()`; `normalizePushPayload()`'s `store_id` probe reads `$target->getTable()`; `AdminStoreDeletionService::storeScopedTables()` and `ForeignKeyCascadeEmulator` take their names from `Schema::getTableListing()`; `BackfillStoreIds::DERIVATIONS` and `AdminStoreOperationalMetricsService::countScoped()` take theirs from server-side constants. Everywhere `push()` compares `$change['table_name']` it is comparing a sync name to a sync name, which is correct. The one remaining structural instance is `CountsSyncedTableRows::serverTableFingerprint()`'s `DB::table($table)`, which is **safe today only because its `AGREEMENT_TABLES` allowlist happens to contain 25 names that are each their own physical table**; rather than add an indirection nothing needs, `SyncPullCursorPhysicalTableTest` now pins that property, so adding a divergent name to the list fails the suite instead of silently comparing two different tables.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** no contract changed — same query, same payload, no new field, nothing for a client to understand.
- **Coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPullCursorPhysicalTableTest.php` — 2 cases. The first removes `_synced_at` from the legacy physical `audit_logs` in the test database (the only way to make a coincidence observable) and was RED before: `Expected: select * from "activity_logs" where "updated_at" > ? / To contain: _synced_at`. The second is the `AGREEMENT_TABLES` guard, green before and after by design — it exists to stay green.

### A-284 — a voided sale's line items were counted by the cloud and never delivered to any device
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-19, server only, three `withTrashed()` calls, TDD.
- **Was:** `SyncController::applyPullTenantScope()` scoped `sale_items`, `return_items` and `sale_item_batches` through parent subqueries built from `Sale::query()`, `SaleReturn::query()` and `SaleItem::query()`. All three parents use `SoftDeletes`, so the global scope removed a soft-deleted parent from the subquery and its children stopped being pulled **at all** — not even as `_deleted: 1`. A device that never received the children's delete kept them indefinitely; a device that never received them at all could never get them. A-176a fixed exactly this shape for `stock_batches` and left a comment in the same `match` asserting the remaining subqueries were deliberately scoped, which is why it went unnoticed. A-281 fixed it for `prescription_items` and `purchase_order_items`.
- **Now:** `tenantSaleIds()` returns `Sale::withTrashed()` (covering `sale_items` and the outer half of `sale_item_batches`), `return_items` scopes through `SaleReturn::withTrashed()`, and the inner `sale_item_batches` subquery through `SaleItem::withTrashed()`. Every child subquery in that `match` is now `withTrashed()` without exception, which is what the comment above it says, so there is no longer a per-table rule to remember.
- **Why it mattered beyond the stale rows:** `CountsSyncedTableRows::scopeToStore()` already counted all three through `withTrashed()` parents, as `DB::table()` plus a trashed-tolerant parent must. So the cloud counted rows the pull refused to send, and the per-table agreement instrument (`docs/DEVICE_TABLE_AGREEMENT.md`) read **device-behind on three tables for every store holding a single voided sale** — which is the common case. Fixing the pull is what makes that verdict trustworthy; suppressing the three tables in the panel would have blinded it in exactly the tables October's data bugs lived in.
- **Cost, which is a one-off re-pull burst per device.** Each device re-pulls the line items of every voided sale and every voided return, once. For a store with a few hundred sales and a typical void rate, that is roughly 20–40 voided sales at ~2–4 items each: on the order of 100 `sale_items`, a similar number of `sale_item_batches`, and a handful of `return_items` — low hundreds of rows, tens of KB, comfortably inside `fetchPullPage()`'s 500-row-per-table page, so at most one extra page per table on one round. It does not scale with catalogue size and it never repeats. A store with tens of thousands of sales would see a few thousand rows spread over a few pages of one round; still bounded, still once.
- **Idempotent on arrival:** these rows take `pull.ts`'s UPDATE-or-INSERT branch like any other, `sale_item_batches` carries no quantity accumulator, and the children arrive carrying `_deleted: 1` from `mapPullRowForClient()`'s `trashed()` check, so a device that already holds them simply re-confirms the tombstone.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** server-side only and additive in effect — the pull sends strictly more rows than before, in the shape every bundle already handles. No new field, no shim, nothing for an old client to understand.
- **Coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPullSoftDeletedParentLineItemsTest.php` — 2 cases, both RED before (`Failed asserting that an array contains '…'` for the sale item; `Failed asserting that 1 is identical to 0` for the count comparison). The second case asserts the pull and `serverTableFingerprint()` return the same row count for all three tables, which is the property that actually has to hold.

### A-282 — the pull watermark could skip rows an in-flight push was about to commit
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-18, server + one client line, TDD. Full reasoning in `laravel-server/AGENTS.md`, "The pull watermark lags".
- **Mechanism confirmed, having been raised as unverified.** `pull()` computed `$serverTimestamp = now()` **before** its SELECTs and returned it as the watermark the client stamps. A push stamps `_synced_at = now()` per change inside its loop and commits the whole batch at the end (`:300`, `:412`, `:664` writing, the outer commit after `applyStockBatchDeltas()`). A pull starting inside that open transaction therefore sees none of those rows and stamps **past** them, and the cursor `_synced_at > W OR updated_at > W` never matches them again. Nothing notices: `checkSyncHealth()` compares counts for five tables, so a lost `stock_movements` row or any lost price/cost **update** changes no count.
- **The second-precision hole is real and independent.** `_synced_at`/`updated_at` are `timestamp` columns (second precision, confirmed in the migrations) and the comparison is strict `>`, so a row committed in the second the pull chose is excluded with no transaction involved at all. Pinned as its own case.
- **Now:** `server_timestamp` is `now()` minus `PULL_WATERMARK_LAG_SECONDS = 60`. **60 is a judgement, not a bound** — an earlier draft of this entry justified it as "PHP's `max_execution_time` caps a push at 30s, so 60 is that doubled", which is **wrong**: on Linux `max_execution_time` counts CPU time (`ITIMER_PROF`) so time blocked in MySQL does not count, and the host is LiteSpeed (`public/.htaccess`, `lsphp`) with no override. The real caps are LiteSpeed's request timeout and `innodb_lock_wait_timeout` (50s default), and because the UPDATE branch takes `lockForUpdate()` **per change**, one contended push can accumulate several 50s lock waits and still commit rows stamped minutes earlier — which the lag does **not** cover. It covers the ordinary case and the whole of the second-precision hole, cheaply. The upper constraint is real (overshooting would have to exceed the sync interval, whose shortest plan value is minutes), so 60 sits safely inside that and usefully but incompletely inside the other. The structural fix — re-stamping `_synced_at` at commit time for every row the push wrote, after which the lag only covers clock skew — is filed as `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md` A-286 and deliberately not implemented here.
- **Idempotency of the extra round, verified in `pull.ts` rather than assumed:** a row already held takes the UPDATE branch and is rewritten with identical values; `pendingLocalEdit` still protects a row with a live `_sync_queue` entry; `stock_batches.quantity` is deleted from the applied columns; a re-pulled `stock_movements` row touches its batch `{0, 0}`, so no delta applies twice.
- **Cost on shared hosting:** a trailing 60s of changes re-offered per round. For a typical till that is its own recent sales — single-digit rows, a few KB, on a round trip that was happening anyway. It does not scale with catalogue size and it is not a full re-pull.
- **The multi-page variant was confirmed and fixed too.** A table's window is stamped on the page that drains it, so a multi-page round stamped the **last** page's timestamp, covering time the earlier pages never queried. Keyset paging orders by `(updated_at, id)` while the filter also admits `_synced_at`, so a row entering the window mid-round with an older `updated_at` than the cursor already passed sorts behind it and is never seen. `pull.ts` now captures `roundTimestamp` from page 1 and stamps that. **It does not interfere with the rewind machinery:** `PULL_PROGRESS.completeWindow` resets `rewind_count`/`rewound_from` whatever value it writes, and `pull-window.ts` restores a stored baseline rather than reasoning about the instant.
- **One existing test pinned the old client behaviour and was corrected:** `pull-paginates-past-500-rows.test.ts` expected the last page's `server_timestamp`; it now expects the first page's, with the reason in place.
- **Backward compatibility (§11):** the server half covers every bundle in the field, which is the point of putting the lag there. An old client stamps what it is given, as always — just a slightly older instant. No new field, no shim. The client half is an improvement on top, not a requirement.
- **Coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPullWatermarkLagTest.php` — 4 cases. RED before on three; the two behavioural ones were re-confirmed RED with the lag set to 0 (the exact pre-fix watermark) so the construction could not be flattering itself: `A row stamped in the watermark's own second must not be excluded for ever` and `A row an open push committed after this pull ran must still reach this device`. The fourth pins that the lag is a trailing window and not a disabled cursor. `client/__tests__/pull-stamps-the-rounds-first-page-timestamp.test.ts` — 2 cases, the multi-page one RED before (`expected "…12:00:00Z", received "…12:04:00Z"`).

### A-230 — closed as not a bug: `stores` is exempt from the pull window entirely
- **Closed:** 2026-10-18, verified in code while fixing A-282, and removed from `KNOWN_BUGS.md`.
- A-230 claimed `syncSubscriptionStatus()`'s `STAMP_STORES_WINDOW_SQL` advances the `stores` window, so the ordinary pull then asks only for stores changed after that stamp and a server-side edit to a non-subscription column in the skipped interval is lost. **The second half does not happen.** `SyncController::applyPullCursor()` opens `if ($lastSynced && $table !== 'stores')`, so the `stores` cursor is never applied: the ordinary pull always fetches every store the caller owns, in full. `fetchPullPage()` exempts `stores` from the 500-row cap in the same breath, and both exemptions are documented in that method's own comment as deliberate, so the client can treat `stores` as a complete snapshot and prune against it.
- So the stamp A-230 objects to is written and never read. It is dead bookkeeping, not data loss, and `__tests__/sync-subscription-status.test.ts`'s "stamps the stores pull cursor with the server timestamp" can stay exactly as it is. Nothing was changed; the entry was wrong about the consequence.

### A-281 — nine models hard-deleted on the server, so deletes never propagated and resends resurrected the row
- **Fixed:** 2026-10-18, server only, TDD. Full reasoning and the complete reader audit in `laravel-server/AGENTS.md`, "Every pulled table soft-deletes".
- **Was:** `Category`, `Expense`, `PurchaseOrder`, `PurchaseOrderItem`, `Prescription`, `PrescriptionItem`, `RequestedProduct`, `SupplierPayment` and `PermissionGroup` were all in `pull()`'s table list and none used `SoftDeletes`, so `push()`'s `$target->delete()` destroyed the row. `mapPullRowForClient()` emits `_deleted` only from `trashed()`, so the row just vanished from every pull: the delete reached no other device, an edit to a destroyed row fell through `if ($model)` and was counted processed, and a whole-row resend recreated it, which then pulled back and **undid the delete on the device that made it**. Live client callers exist for `expenses` (`use-expense-mutations.ts`), `categories` (`queries/categories.ts`), `purchase_orders`/`purchase_order_items` (`procurement.ts`), `prescription_items` (`queries/prescriptions.ts`), `requested_products` and `permission_groups` (`use-permission-groups.ts`). Expenses are money in the P&L, so two tills disagreeing about them is an accounting defect, not a cosmetic one.
- **The finding that raised this was wrong about one thing:** it stated all nine tables already had a `deleted_at` column. **`permission_groups` did not** (verified against the migrated schema), and it is one of the tables the client really does delete — so this change carries `2026_10_18_000001_add_deleted_at_to_permission_groups_table.php`. The other eight needed no migration. `customer_payments` also lacks the column, but nothing deletes it and it was not in the nine.
- **Now:** all nine use the trait. Eight readers were changed with it, because the global scope silently rewrites every existing query on those models — the full list and the reasoning for each is in the `AGENTS.md` section. The two load-bearing ones: `applyPullTenantScope()`'s child subqueries (a soft-deleted parent otherwise stops its children being pulled at all, so no device could ever learn they were deleted — A-176a's rule) and `findDuplicateInsertConflict()` (`categories` is `UNIQUE (store_id, name)` and that index counts soft-deleted rows, so a probe blind to a trashed row attempts the INSERT and the push fails on the constraint; the caller now `restore()`s a trashed row it remaps onto, or the owner's re-created category would arrive `_deleted: 1` and vanish again). `CrossTenantCategoryRepairService` had *already* written a `whereNotNull('deleted_at')` branch for exactly this collision, and the global scope would have made that branch unreachable while leaving the collision — it needed `withTrashed()` to keep doing what it says.
